print(next_depth)\n    # raise NotImplementedError\n    return next_X, next_depth",
    "Experiment Result": "The core methodology involves two main components: Focalized GP and FocalBO.\n\n**Focalized GP (implemented by `SVGP` and `train_fvgp` using `FocalizedVariationalELBO`):**\n*   **Model (`SVGP`):** A sparse Gaussian Process model (ApproximateGP) using a CholeskyVariationalDistribution and VariationalStrategy with learnable inducing locations.\n*   **Loss Function (`FocalizedVariationalELBO`):** A novel variational loss that extends `VariationalELBO`.\n    *   **Likelihood weighting:** The data likelihood term (`_log_likelihood_term`) is not summed initially. Instead, a `weight` is computed for each training point (`train_x`) based on its kernel covariance to the `test_distribution` (representing the search region).\n        *   Specifically, `weight[diff>0] = torch.diagonal(K_xt)` where `K_xt = self.model.covar_module(train_x[diff>0], test_distribution[diff>0])`. The weights are then normalized.\n    *   **Regularization:** An additional `weight_loss` term (calculated as `(mll.weight).sum() / num_in_region - 1` or `(mll.weight).sum()`) is added to the ELBO loss during training if `reg` is enabled. This term encourages accurate local prediction.\n*   **Training (`train_fvgp`):**\n    *   `inducing_points` are initialized using a Sobol sequence, optionally within the `test_distribution` bounds, or centered around points with enough data coverage.\n    *   Training involves `num_epochs` iterations with an Adam optimizer.\n\n**FocalBO (implemented by `focal_acqf_opt_sample`):**\n*   **Hierarchical Optimization:** The algorithm iteratively optimizes the acquisition function over `max_loop_num` progressively smaller search regions.\n    *   At each `loop` iteration, the `state.length` (search space length) is halved (`state.length /= 2`).\n*   **Search Region Re-centering:** `x_center` is initially `0.5 * torch.ones(...)` or derived from the best point in `train_loader.dataset.tensors[1].argmax()`. In subsequent iterations, `x_center` is typically re-centered at the current best observed position (from `train_x[train_loader.dataset.tensors[1].argmax()]`). The search region bounds (`tr_lb`, `tr_ub`) are dynamically adjusted around `x_center` using `weights` (derived from the model's lengthscales or as ones) and `state.length`.\n*   **Acquisition Function Optimization:** In each loop, an acquisition function (e.g., Expected Improvement, UCB, Thompson Sampling) is optimized over the current search region to propose a `batch_size` of new inputs (`new_x`).\n*   **Final Decision Sampling:** A batch of `batch_size` inputs (`next_X`) is sampled from all generated candidates (`new_X`) across all loops. This sampling is performed using a Softmax distribution over their acquisition values (`select_prob = torch.softmax(base_temp * new_Acq, dim=0)`), ensuring selection is weighted by acquisition value.\n*   **Adaptive Optimization Depth:** If `args.auto == 1`, the `max_loop_num` (optimization depth H) is adaptively adjusted based on the `depth` (`loop`) from which the best-performing proposed point (`new_Y.argmax()`) was sampled. If the best point is from a shallower depth, `max_loop_num` is decreased; otherwise, it's increased (`if depth + 1 < max_loop_num: max_loop_num -= 1 else: max_loop_num += 1`)."

    "Title": "Efficient Nonmyopic Bayesian Optimization via One-Shot Multi-Step Trees",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper provides the first efficient implementation of general multi-step lookahead Bayesian optimization (BO), addressing the intractability of computing full lookahead policies. Its main contributions include a novel 'one-shot' multi-step tree-based acquisition function that jointly optimizes all decision variables, an efficient multi-step Gaussian process 'fantasization' method leveraging linear algebra insights and parallel computation for fast cache updates, and demonstrated superior optimization performance over existing myopic and nonmyopic methods on a range of benchmarks while maintaining competitive wall times. The work also introduces two scalable special cases: Multi-Step Path and Non-Adaptive Approximation (ENO).",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology involves approximating the intractable k-step BO problem using a differentiable, tree-based acquisition function. Instead of nested optimization, all decision variables within a multi-step scenario tree are optimized jointly in a 'one-shot' fashion, enabled by the reparameterization trick to express fantasy samples using independent base samples. The efficiency is further boosted by 'multi-step fast fantasies,' which employ novel linear algebra methods to efficiently update GPyTorch’s LOVE caches for conditioning GP models and leverage batched tensor operations for parallelization. The optimization uses gradient-based methods (L-BFGS-B) with auto-differentiation, enhanced by random restarting and a warm-start initialization strategy. Two scalable special cases, 'Multi-Step Path' (single future path) and 'Non-Adaptive Approximation' (ENO, non-adaptive decisions approximated by a one-step batch value function), are also proposed.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The algorithms are implemented in BoTorch and GPyTorch, utilizing a Gaussian Process with a constant mean and a Matérn 5/2 ARD kernel, with GP hyperparameters re-estimated after each iteration. Experiments begin with 2d random initial observations and perform 20d iterations of BO, with 100 repeats for each method and function. Performance is measured using GAP = (yi - y0) / (y* - y0). Benchmarks include nine 'hard' synthetic functions (e.g., eggholder, dropwave, shubert) and seven real hyperparameter tuning functions (e.g., SVM, LDA, logistic regression, neural network tuning on Boston Housing and Breast Cancer datasets, robot pushing). Comparisons are made against one-step EI, a BINOCULARS variant (12.EI.s), and Envelope Two-Step (ETS), as well as the proposed k-step (k=2,3,4), k-path, and k-ENO methods. Experiments are run on CPU Linux machines using a single core.",
    "Limitations": "The general one-shot optimization problem's dimension grows exponentially with the lookahead horizon (k), limiting practical application to relatively small horizons (e.g., less than 5). There are diminishing returns in performance with increasing horizon beyond 3-step, potentially due to the increased complexity of optimizing the objective or a greater reliance on model accuracy, which may not hold in practice. The paper also highlights a limitation of a related two-step method (ETS) by Wu & Frazier, which relies on an assumption of global optima for second-stage value functions that can be unrealistic and lead to discontinuity, a challenge our method aims to mitigate.",
    "Future Research Directions": "A suggested future research direction is to investigate the application of the developed framework to other problems, such as Bayesian quadrature.",

    "Title": "Local policy search with Bayesian optimization",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper addresses the challenge of high sample complexity and variance in reinforcement learning (RL) policy search, where policy gradient methods often rely on random perturbations. It proposes Gradient Information with Bayesian Optimization (GIBO), a novel local policy search method that combines gradient-based optimization with active sampling. GIBO utilizes a Gaussian Process (GP) model of the objective function and its gradient to actively select informative queries, thereby optimally reducing uncertainty about the Jacobian. Key contributions include significantly improved sample complexity and reduced variance in extensive empirical evaluations on synthetic objectives, as well as solving popular RL benchmarks in a more sample-efficient manner with reduced reward variance compared to non-active sampling baselines.",
    "Methodology": "The policy search problem is framed as a zeroth-order black-box optimization problem. GIBO models the objective function and its Jacobian (gradient) jointly using a Gaussian Process (GP), leveraging the property that the derivative of a GP is also a GP. A novel acquisition function, 'Gradient Information (GI)', is introduced to actively suggest query points that maximize the expected reduction in the Jacobian's variance at the current parameters. The GIBO algorithm consists of two loops: an outer loop for iterative parameter updates (e.g., gradient ascent using the GP's posterior gradient estimate) and an inner loop where the GI acquisition function queries points to increase gradient information. Implementation choices include a local GP model (using only the most recent samples), local optimization of the acquisition function within bounded search spaces, gradient normalization using the Mahalanobis norm with GP lengthscales, and state normalization for RL environments.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The performance of GIBO was evaluated in three types of experiments. First, a 'within-model comparison' was conducted on synthetic objective functions sampled from a known GP prior, covering 4 to 36 dimensions. GIBO was compared against Augmented Random Search (ARS), Covariance Matrix Adaptation Evolution Strategy (CMA-ES), and standard Bayesian Optimization (BO) with expected improvement. Each run had a budget of 300 noisy function evaluations. Second, GIBO was applied to a challenging Linear Quadratic Regulator (LQR) problem with unknown dynamics, comparing its performance to ARS and Least-Squares Policy Iteration (LSPI). Finally, GIBO was tested on classic control (CartPole-v1) and MuJoCo locomotion tasks (Swimmer-v1, Hopper-v1) from OpenAI Gym, where it was compared to ARS using deterministic linear policies. Metrics across experiments included normalized distance from the true global maximum (for synthetic), frequency of stable controllers and relative cost (for LQR), and average reward over function evaluations with standard deviation (for Gym/MuJoCo). An ablation study investigated the impact of gradient and state normalization.",
    "Limitations": "The performance benefits of GIBO are less pronounced in real-world RL tasks where the strict model assumptions, such as the objective function being a sample from a known GP prior, do not hold exactly. The authors note that prior knowledge can bias gradient estimates, and wrong assumptions about the objective function can lead to performance deterioration. Furthermore, the paper does not discuss how the autonomous decision-making process of GIBO can be constrained to ensure compliance with regulatory requirements, which is a relevant aspect for future research concerning legal and ethical consequences.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include extending GIBO to integrate with state-of-the-art policy gradient methods (e.g., actor-critic algorithms) as an additional oracle for first-order information. This integration aims to leverage GIBO's active sampling capabilities to further reduce the variance of gradient estimates and improve sample complexity in these advanced RL algorithms. Another critical area for future work is to investigate how GIBO, as an autonomous decision-maker, can be constrained to ensure compliance with regulatory and ethical requirements, addressing the broader implications of automated learning experiments.",
    "Experiment Code": "File Path: src/acquisition_function.py\nContent:\nfrom typing import Tuple\n\nimport torch\nimport gpytorch\nimport botorch\n\nfrom src.cholesky import one_step_cholesky\n\n\nclass GradientInformation(botorch.acquisition.AnalyticAcquisitionFunction):\n    '''Acquisition function to sample points for gradient information.\n\n    Attributes:\n        model: Gaussian process model that supplies the Jacobian (e.g. DerivativeExactGPSEModel).\n    '''\n\n    def __init__(self, model):\n        '''Inits acquisition function with model.'''\n        super().__init__(model)\n\n    def update_theta_i(self, theta_i: torch.Tensor):\n        '''Updates the current parameters.\n\n        This leads to an update of K_xX_dx.\n\n        Args:\n            theta_i: New parameters.\n        '''\n        if not torch.is_tensor(theta_i):\n            theta_i = torch.tensor(theta_i)\n        self.theta_i = theta_i\n        self.update_K_xX_dx()\n\n    def update_K_xX_dx(self):\n        '''When new x is given update K_xX_dx.'''\n        # Pre-compute large part of K_xX_dx.\n        X = self.model.train_inputs[0]\n        x = self.theta_i.view(-1, self.model.D)\n        self.K_xX_dx_part = self._get_KxX_dx(x, X)\n\n    def _get_KxX_dx(self, x, X) -> torch.Tensor:\n        '''Computes the analytic derivative of the kernel K(x,X) w.r.t. x.\n\n        Args:\n            x: (n x D) Test points.\n\n        Returns:\n            (n x D) The derivative of K(x,X) w.r.t. x.\n        '''\n        N = X.shape[0]\n        n = x.shape[0]\n        K_xX = self.model.covar_module(x, X).evaluate()\n        lengthscale = self.model.covar_module.base_kernel.lengthscale.detach()\n        return (\n            -torch.eye(self.model.D, device=X.device)\n            / lengthscale ** 2\n            @ (\n                (x.view(n, 1, self.model.D) - X.view(1, N, self.model.D))\n                * K_xX.view(n, N, 1)\n            ).transpose(1, 2)\n        )\n\n    # TODO: nicer batch-update for batch of thetas.\n    @botorch.utils.transforms.t_batch_mode_transform(expected_q=1)\n    def forward(self, thetas: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:\n        '''Evaluate the acquisition function on the candidate set thetas.\n\n        Args:\n            thetas: A (b) x D-dim Tensor of (b) batches with a d-dim theta points each.\n\n        Returns:\n            A (b)-dim Tensor of acquisition function values at the given theta points.\n        '''\n        sigma_n = self.model.likelihood.noise_covar.noise\n        D = self.model.D\n        X = self.model.train_inputs[0]\n        x = self.theta_i.view(-1, D)\n\n        variances = []\n        for theta in thetas:\n            theta = theta.view(-1, D)\n            # Compute K_Xθ, K_θθ (do not forget to add noise).\n            K_Xθ = self.model.covar_module(X, theta).evaluate()\n            K_θθ = self.model.covar_module(theta).evaluate() + sigma_n * torch.eye(\n                K_Xθ.shape[-1]\n            ).to(theta)\n\n            # Get Cholesky factor.\n            L = one_step_cholesky(\n                top_left=self.model.get_L_lower().transpose(-1, -2),\n                K_Xθ=K_Xθ,\n                K_θθ=K_θθ,\n                A_inv=self.model.get_KXX_inv(),\n            )\n\n            # Get K_XX_inv.\n            K_XX_inv = torch.cholesky_inverse(L, upper=True)\n\n            # get K_xX_dx\n            K_xθ_dx = self._get_KxX_dx(x, theta)\n            K_xX_dx = torch.cat([self.K_xX_dx_part, K_xθ_dx], dim=-1)\n\n            # Compute_variance.\n            variance_d = -K_xX_dx @ K_XX_inv @ K_xX_dx.transpose(1, 2)\n            variances.append(torch.trace(variance_d.view(D, D)).view(1))\n\n        return -torch.cat(variances, dim=0)\n\n\ndef optimize_acqf_custom_bo(\n    acq_func: botorch.acquisition.AcquisitionFunction,\n    bounds: torch.Tensor,\n    q: int,\n    num_restarts: int,\n    raw_samples: int,\n) -> Tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:\n    '''Function to optimize the GradientInformation acquisition function for custom Bayesian optimization.\n\n    Args:\n        acq_function: An AcquisitionFunction.\n        bounds: A 2 x D tensor of lower and upper bounds for each column of X.\n        q: The number of candidates.\n        num_restarts: The number of starting points for multistart acquisition function optimization.\n        raw_samples: The number of samples for initialization.\n\n    Returns:\n        A two-element tuple containing:\n            - a q x D-dim tensor of generated candidates.\n            - a tensor of associated acquisition values.\n    '''\n    candidates, acq_value = botorch.optim.optimize_acqf(\n        acq_function=acq_func,\n        bounds=bounds,\n        q=q,\n        num_restarts=num_restarts,\n        raw_samples=raw_samples,\n        options={'nonnegative': True, 'batch_limit': 5},\n        return_best_only=True,\n        sequential=False,\n    )\n    new_x = candidates.detach()\n    return new_x, acq_value\n\nFile Path: src/cholesky.py\nContent:\nimport torch\n\n\ndef one_step_cholesky(\n    top_left: torch.Tensor, K_Xθ: torch.Tensor, K_θθ: torch.Tensor, A_inv: torch.Tensor\n) -> torch.Tensor:\n    '''Update the Cholesky factor when the matrix is extended.\n\n    Note: See thesis appendix A.2 for notation of args and further information.\n\n    Args:\n        top_left: Cholesky factor L11 of old matrix A11.\n        K_Xθ: Upper right bock matrix A12 of new matrix A.\n        K_θθ: Lower right block matrix A22 of new matrix A.\n        A_inv: Inverse of old matrix A11.\n\n    Returns:\n        New cholesky factor S of new matrix A.\n    '''\n    top_right = top_left @ (A_inv @ K_Xθ)\n    bot_left = torch.zeros_like(top_right).transpose(-1, -2)\n    bot_right = torch.cholesky(\n        K_θθ - top_right.transpose(-1, -2) @ top_right, upper=True\n    )\n    return torch.cat(\n        [\n            torch.cat([top_left, top_right], dim=-1),\n            torch.cat([bot_left, bot_right], dim=-1),\n        ],\n        dim=-2,\n    )\n\nFile Path: src/model.py\nContent:\nimport torch\nimport gpytorch\nimport botorch\n\n\nclass def forward(self, x):\n        \"\"\"Compute the prior latent distribution on a given input.\n\n        Typically, this will involve a mean and kernel function. The result must be a\n        MultivariateNormal. Calling this model will return the posterior of the latent\n        Gaussian process when conditioned on the training data. The output will be a\n        MultivariateNormal.\n\n        Args:\n            x: (n x D) The test points.\n\n        Returns:\n            A MultivariateNormal.\n        \"\"\"\n        mean_x = \"\"\"\n        L_inv_upper = self.prediction_strategy.covar_cache.detach()\n        return L_inv_upper @ L_inv_upper.transpose(0, 1)\n\n    def _get_KxX_dx(self, x):\n        \"\"\"Computes the      X = self.train_inputs[0]\n        n = x.shape[0]\n        K_xX = self.covar_module(x, X).evaluate()\n        lengthscale = self.covar_module.base_kernel.lengthscale.detach()\n        return (\n            -torch.eye(self.D, device=x.device)\n            / lengthscale ** 2\n            @ (\n                (x.view(n, 1, self.D) - X.view(1, self.N, self.D))\n                * K_xX.view(n, self.N, 1)\n            ).transpose(1, 2)\n        )\n\n    def _get_Kxx_dx2(self):\n        \"\"\"Computes the analytic second derivative of the kernel K(x,x) w.r.t. x.\n\n        Args:\n ** 2\n        ) * sigma_f\n\n    def posterior_derivative(self, x):\n        \"\"\"Computes the posterior of the derivative of the GP w.r.t. the given test\n        points x.\n\n        Args:\n StateNormalizer(StateManipulator):\n    \"\"\"Class for state normalization.\n\n    Implementation of Welfords online algorithm. For further information see\n        thesis appendix A.3.\n\n    Attributes:\n        eps: Small value to prevent division by zero\n        normalize_params: Normalization function for policy parameters.\n        unnormalize_params: Unnormalization function for policy parameters.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(\n        self, eps: float = 1e-8, normalize_params=None, unnormalize_params=None\n    ):\n        # Init super.\n        self.eps = eps\n        self.steps = 0\n        self._mean_of_states = 0.0\n        self._sum_of_squared_errors = 0.0\n        self.mean = 0.0\n        self.std = 1.0\n        if normalize_params is None:\n            normalize_params = lambda params, mean, std: params\n        self.__normalize_params = normalize_params\n\n        if unnormalize_params is None:\n            unnormalize_params = lambda params, mean, std: params\n        self.__unnormalize_params = unnormalize_params\n\n    def _get_mean_var(self) -> Tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:\n        \"\"\"Helper function for apply_update.\n\n        Returns:\n            Means and variances of states.\n        \"\"\"\n        if self.steps <= 1:\n            var = torch.ones_like(self._mean_of_states)\n        else:\n            # Sample variance.\n            var = self._sum_of_squared_errors / (self.steps - 1)\n        return self._mean_of_states, var\n\n    def _welford_update(self, state: torch.Tensor):\n        \"\"\"Helper function for manipulate.\n\n        Internally trackes mean and std according to the seen states.\n\n        Args:\n            state: New state.\n        \"\"\"\n        # https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algorithms_for_calculating_variance#Welford's_online_algorithm\n        self.steps += 1\n        delta = state - self._mean_of_states\n        self._mean_of_states += delta / self.steps\n        self._sum_of_squared_errors += delta * (state - self._mean_of_states)\n\n    def manipulate(self, state: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:\n        \"\"\"Actually manipulate a state with the tracked mean and standard\n        deviation.\n\n        Args:\n            state: State to normalize.\n\n        Returns:\n            Normalized state.\n        \"\"\"\n        self._welford_update(state)\n        normalized_state = (state - self.mean) / self.std\n        return normalized_state\n\n    def apply_update(self):\n        \"\"\"Updates mean and std according to the states internally tracked using\n        _welford_update.\"\"\"\n        self.mean, var = self._get_mean_var()\n        self.std = torch.sqrt(var) + self.eps\n\n    def normalize_params(self, params: torch.Tensor):\n        return self.__normalize_params(params, self.mean, self.std)\n\n    def unnormalize_params(self, params: torch.Tensor):\n        return self.__unnormalize_params(params, self.mean, self.std)\n\n\ndef manipulate_reward(shift: Union[int, float], scale: Union[int, float]):\n    \"\"\"Manipulate reward in every step with shift and scale.\n\n    Args:\n        shift: Reward shift.\n        scale: Reward scale.\n\n    Return:\n        Manipulated reward.\n    \"\"\"\n    if shift is None:\n        shift = 0\n    if scale is None:\n        scale = 1\n    return lambda reward, action, state, done: (reward - shift) / scale\n\nFile Path: src/policy_parameterizations.py\nContent:\nfrom typing import Tuple, List, Callable, Union, Optional\n\nimport torch\n\n\nclass MLP:\n    \"\"\"Multilayer perceptrone.\n\n    Consists of at least two layers of nodes: an input layer and an output\n    layer. Optionally one can extend it with arbitrary many hidden layers.\n    Except for the input nodes, each node is a neuron that can optionally use a\n    nonlinear activation function.\n\n    Attributes:\n        L0: Number of input nodes. For a gym environment objective this\n            corresponds to the states.\n        Ls: List of numbers for nodes of optional hidden layers and the output\n            layer. For a gym environment objective the last number of the list\n            has to correspond to the actions.\n        add_bias: If True every layer has one bias vector of the same dimension\n            as the output dimension of the layer.\n        nonlinearity: Opportunity to hand over a nonlinearity function.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    def __init__(\n        self,\n        L0: int,\n        *Ls: List[int],\n        add_bias: bool = False,\n        nonlinearity: Optional[Callable[[torch.Tensor], torch.Tensor]] = None,\n    ):\n        \"\"\"Inits MLP.\"\"\"\n        self.L0 = L0\n        self.Ls = Ls\n        self.add_bias = add_bias\n        self.len_params = sum(\n            [\n                (in_size + 1 * add_bias) * out_size\n                for in_size, out_size in zip((L0,) + Ls[:-1], Ls)\n            ]\n        )\n\n        if nonlinearity is None:\n            nonlinearity = lambda x: x\n        self.nonlinearity = nonlinearity\n\n    def __call__(self, state: torch.Tensor, params: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:\n        \"\"\"Maps states and parameters of MLP to its actions.\n\n        Args:\n            state: The state tensor.\n            params: Parameters of the MLP.\n\n        Returns:\n            Output of the MLP/actions.\n        \"\"\"\n        with torch.no_grad():\n            params = params.view(self.len_params)\n            out = state\n            start, end = (0, 0)\n            in_size = self.L0\n            for out_size in self.Ls:\n                # Linear mapping.\n                start, end = end, end + in_size * out_size\n                out = out @ params[start:end].view(in_size, out_size)\n                # Add bias.\n                if self.add_bias:\n                    start, end = end, end + out_size\n                    out = out + params[start:end]\n                # Apply nonlinearity.\n                out = self.nonlinearity(out)\n                in_size = out_size\n        return out\n\n    def _select_first_layer(\n        self, params: torch.Tensor\n    ) -> Tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:\n        \"\"\"Helper function for state normalization.\n\n        Normalization is only applied for the first layer out = A @ in + b.\n\n        Args:\n            params: Parameters of MLP.\n\n        Returns:\n            A tuple of the layer nodes A and its biases b.\n\n        Raises:\n            ValueError: If the mlp has no bias vectors in every layer.\n        \"\"\"\n        if self.add_bias is False:\n            raise ValueError(\n                f\"For state normalization the MLP should have biases, but add_bias is {self.add_bias}.\"\n            )\n        in_size = self.L0\n        out_size = self.Ls[0]\n        A = params[:, : in_size * out_size].view(-1, in_size, out_size)\n        b = params[:, in_size * out_size : (in_size + 1) * out_size]\n        return A, b\n\n    def normalize_params(\n        self,\n        params: torch.Tensor,\n        mean: Union[float, torch.Tensor],\n        std: Union[float, torch.Tensor],\n    ) -> torch.Tensor:\n        \"\"\"State normalization for a MLP.\n\n        Only the first layer is transformed affine linear.\n        For further information see thesis 4.2 Extensions.\n\n        Args:\n            params: Parameters of MLP.\n            mean: Mean of states.\n            std: Standard deviation of states.\n\n        Returns:\n            Normalized parameters of MLP.\n        \"\"\"\n        params = params.clone()\n        A, b = self._select_first_layer(params)\n        if type(mean) is torch.Tensor:\n            b += mean @ A\n        else:\n            b += (mean * torch.ones(A.shape[1])) @ A\n\n        if type(std) is torch.Tensor:\n            A *= std.view(-1, 1)\n        else:\n            A *= std\n\n        return params\n\n    def unnormalize_params(\n        self,\n        params: torch.Tensor,\n        mean: Union[float, torch.Tensor],\n        std: Union[float, torch.Tensor],\n    ) -> torch.Tensor:\n        \"\"\"State unnormalization for a MLP.\n\n        Only the first layer is transformed affine linear.\n        For further information see thesis 4.2 Extensions.\n\n        Args:\n            params: Parameters of MLP.\n            mean: Mean of states.\n            std: Standard deviation of states.\n\n        Returns:\n            Unnormalized parameters of MLP.\n        \"\"\"\n        params = params.clone()\n        A, b = self._select_first_layer(params)\n        if type(std) is torch.Tensor:\n            A /= std.view(-1, 1)\n        else:\n            A /= std\n\n        if type(mean) is torch.Tensor:\n            b -= mean @ A\n        else:\n            b -= (mean * torch.ones(A.shape[1])) @ A\n\n        return params\n\n\ndef discretize(function: Callable, num_actions: int):\n    \"\"\"Discretize output/actions of MLP.\n\n    For instance necessary for the CartPole environment.\n\n    Args:\n        function: Mapping states with parameters to actions, e.g. MLP.\n        num_actions: Number of function outputs.\n\n    Returns:\n        Function with num_actions discrete outputs.\n    \"\"\"\n\n    def discrete_policy_2(state, params):\n        return (function(state, params) > 0.0) * 1\n\n    def discrete_policy_n(state, params):\n        return torch.argmax(function(state, params))\n\n    if num_actions == 2:\n        discrete_policy = discrete_policy_2\n    elif num_actions > 2:\n        discrete_policy = discrete_policy_n\n    else:\n        raise (f\"Argument num_actions is {num_actions} but has to be greater than 1.\")\n    return discrete_policy",
    "Experiment Result": "GIBO (BayesianGradientAscent) algorithm experimental settings are configured through various parameters. The core model is a `DerivativeExactGPSEModel`, which models the objective function and its Jacobian jointly using a Gaussian Process. This GP model can be configured to use a `N_max` parameter to limit the number of most recent training samples, effectively creating a local GP model.\n\nThe algorithm's acquisition function is `GradientInformation`, which suggests query points by maximizing the expected reduction in the Jacobian's variance. The optimization of this acquisition function uses `num_restarts` and `raw_samples` for initialization heuristics.\n\nThe search space for the acquisition function's optimization can be bounded, defined by either explicit `bounds` or dynamically by a `delta` value around the current parameters. An `epsilon_diff_acq_value` can be set as a stopping criterion for the inner loop of acquisition function queries, halting sampling if the acquisition value improvement falls below this threshold within `max_samples_per_iteration`.\n\nGradient estimation from the GP posterior can be modified via two flags:\n*   `normalize_gradient`: Normalizes the gradient estimate by its L2 norm and scales it by the GP's lengthscales.\n*   `standard_deviation_scaling`: Scales the gradient by the diagonal elements of the posterior variance of the gradient.\n\nFor reinforcement learning (RL) environments, `StateNormalizer` can be enabled to normalize incoming states. This normalizer updates its mean and standard deviation of states online using Welford's algorithm and applies corresponding transformations to the first layer parameters of the policy MLP through `normalize_params` and `unnormalize_params` methods, which are passed to the `DerivativeExactGPSEModel`.\n\nHyperparameters of the GP model (lengthscale, outputscale, noise) can optionally be optimized or fixed, with options to specify priors and constraints. Learning rate for parameter updates can be managed by a `lr_schedular`. Initial training data for the GP can be generated via `generate_initial_data`."

    "Title": "Multi-Fidelity Bayesian Optimization via Deep Neural Networks",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper proposes Deep Neural Network Multi-Fidelity Bayesian Optimization (DNN-MFBO) to address the inefficiency of existing multi-fidelity BO methods that either ignore or oversimplify complex correlations across fidelities. DNN-MFBO flexibly captures complicated, possibly nonlinear and nonstationary, relationships between fidelities using stacked neural networks to improve objective function estimation and optimization performance. It also introduces a tractable and efficient mutual information-based acquisition function computed via sequential, fidelity-wise Gauss-Hermite quadrature and moment-matching.",
    "Methodology": "DNN-MFBO uses a stacked neural network architecture where each NN models one fidelity. For fidelity m > 1, the NN input includes both the original objective input and the output from the previous fidelity (fm-1(x)). The output layer weights (wm) of each NN are treated as random variables with a standard normal prior, while other weights (θm) are hyper-parameters. A stochastic variational learning algorithm is developed to jointly estimate the posterior of random weights (q(wm) = N(wm|µm, Σm)) and hyper-parameters by maximizing an ELBO. To compute the acquisition function, fidelity-wise moment matching and Gauss-Hermite quadrature approximate the output posteriors p(fm(x)|D) as Gaussian distributions. A mutual information-based acquisition function (Max-Value Entropy Search, MES) is used, requiring Monte-Carlo approximation for the conditional entropy term and further moment matching to approximate conditional posteriors as Gaussian for analytical entropy calculation. Automatic differentiation and L-BFGS are used for acquisition function optimization.",
    "Experimental Setup": "DNN-MFBO was evaluated on three synthetic benchmark datasets: Branin function (3 fidelities, 2D input), Park1 function (2 fidelities, 4D input), and Levy function (3 fidelities, 2D input). It was also tested on two real-world engineering design applications: Mechanical Plate Vibration Design (3 material properties, 2 fidelities) and Thermal Conductor Design (3 shape parameters, 2 fidelities). Competing methods included MF-SKO, MF-GP-UCB, MF-PES, MF-MES, MTNN-BO, and SF-MES. Evaluation metrics were simple regret (SR), inference regret (IR), and the queried maximum/minimum function values, along with query cost and average query time. Neural network architectures were identified using SMAC3 and manual tuning; ADAM optimizer was used for training.",
    "Limitations": "The paper primarily highlights the limitations of *prior* multi-fidelity BO methods (e.g., ignoring or oversimplifying fidelity correlations, computational intractability with complex kernels). While the proposed method aims to overcome these, the authors note that their work focuses on *discrete* fidelities, contrasting with some algorithms that deal with continuous fidelities. The calculation of the acquisition function is described as \"quite complex,\" though the authors claim tractability and efficiency due to analytical forms and automatic differentiation.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Not mentioned",
    "Experiment Code": "if __name__ == '__main__':    rx = float(sys.argv[1])    ry = float(sys.argv[2])    gx = float(sys.argv[4])    gy = float(sys.argv[5])    simu_steps = int(float(sys.argv[3]) * 10)        world = b2WorldInterface(False)    oshape, osize, ofriction, odensity, bfriction, hand_shape, hand_size  = 'circle', 1, 0.01, 0.05, 0.01, 'rectangle', (0.3,1)     thing,base = make_thing(500, 500, world, oshape, osize, ofriction, odensity, bfriction, (0,0))    init_angle = np.arctan(ry/rx)    robot = end_effector(world, (rx,ry), base, init_angle, hand_shape, hand_size)    ret = simu_push(world, thing, robot, base, simu_steps)    ret = np.linalg.norm(np.array([gx, gy]) -     "Experiment Result": "The experimental setup involves a physics-based 'push world' simulation, implemented using the Box2D library, which serves as the objective function for optimization. The goal of the simulation is to optimize robot pushing actions to move an object to a predefined target location. Key input parameters that can be varied (and thus optimized by DNN-MFBO) include: robot initial position (`rx`, `ry`), target object position (`gx`, `gy`), and `simu_steps`. The `simu_steps` parameter directly controls the duration and, consequently, the fidelity of the simulation (e.g., `int(float(sys.argv[3]) * 10)`). A higher `simu_steps` value corresponds to a longer and potentially more accurate (higher-fidelity) simulation run, which is computationally more expensive. This aligns with DNN-MFBO's multi-fidelity approach. Other adjustable parameters include the initial angle of the robot's push (`init_angle`), specific push velocities (`xvel`, `yvel` in other variants), and various physical properties of the object being pushed and the robot's hand, such as shape (`oshape`, `hand_shape`), size (`osize`, `hand_size`), friction (`ofriction`), and density (`odensity`). The objective function, which DNN-MFBO aims to minimize, is the Euclidean distance between the final resting position of the pushed object and its specified target position."

    "Title": "Efficient Hyperparameter Optimization with Adaptive Fidelity Identification",
    "Main Contributions": "FastBO addresses the challenge in multi-fidelity Bayesian optimization (BO) of adaptively determining the appropriate fidelity for each hyperparameter configuration to fit the surrogate model, a problem existing methods struggle with due to assumptions about learning curves or use of fixed fidelities. FastBO introduces the novel concepts of \"efficient point\" and \"saturation point\" to enable adaptive fidelity identification, leading to efficient and strong performance in hyperparameter optimization (HPO) and neural architecture search (NAS). It also demonstrates that this adaptive fidelity identification strategy can generalize to extend any single-fidelity method to the multi-fidelity setting.",
    "Methodology": "FastBO extends Bayesian Optimization by adaptively identifying the optimal fidelity for each configuration. It defines two key concepts: the \"efficient point\" (ei = min{r | Ci(r) \n Ci(2r) < δ1}), which signifies the fidelity offering an optimal resource-to-performance balance and is used to fit the surrogate model, and the \"saturation point\" (si = min{r | ∀r′ > r,|Ci(r′)−Ci(r)| < δ2}), which indicates when a configuration's performance stabilizes. The FastBO process involves a warm-up stage, learning curve estimation to extract these points, evaluation up to the efficient point for surrogate model updates, and a post-processing stage where promising configurations are resumed to their saturation points. This adaptive fidelity identification strategy is also proposed as a general method to extend single-fidelity optimization techniques to multi-fidelity settings.",
    "Experimental Setup": "FastBO's performance was evaluated against several multi-fidelity and single-fidelity HPO/NAS methods, including Random Search (RS), standard BO, ASHA, Hyperband, PASHA, A-BOHB, A-CQR, BOHB, DyHPO, and Hyper-Tune.     "Title": "Batch Multi-Fidelity Bayesian Optimization with  Deep Auto-Regressive Networks",
    "Main Contributions": "BMBO-DARN addresses the limitations of existing multi-fidelity Bayesian optimization (BO) by proposing a deep auto-regressive model and an efficient batch querying method. The deep auto-regressive network uses Bayesian neural networks to capture complex, strong, and potentially nonlinear relationships across different fidelities, improving surrogate learning. A novel batch acquisition function, based on the Max-value Entropy Search (MES) principle, is developed to encourage query diversity and penalize highly correlated queries while balancing benefit and cost. An alternating optimization algorithm is introduced to efficiently maximize this batch acquisition function, avoiding a combinatorial search. The method demonstrates superior performance and cost-efficiency in various real-world hyperparameter optimization tasks.",
    "Methodology": "The core methodology relies on a deep auto-regressive model for multi-fidelity surrogate learning. It consists of a chain of Bayesian neural networks (BNNs), where each fidelity (from 1 to M) is modeled by a BNN. The input to the BNN for fidelity 'm' includes the original objective function input 'x' concatenated with the outputs from all previous fidelities (f1(x), ..., fm-1(x)). Hamiltonian Monte Carlo (HMC) sampling is used for posterior inference of the BNN weights, providing robust uncertainty quantification. For batch querying, a batch acquisition function is proposed, based on the Max-value Entropy Search (MES) principle, which computes the mutual information between a batch of potential query outputs and the function optimum, divided by their total cost. This acquisition function is efficiently computed by approximating the posterior distribution of the batch outputs and the optimum as a multivariate Gaussian, estimated through posterior samples of NN weights and moment matching. To optimize this batch acquisition function over continuous inputs and discrete fidelities, an alternating optimization algorithm is employed, iteratively updating one (input, fidelity) pair at a time while fixing the others, which guarantees improvement at each step and avoids computationally expensive combinatorial searches.",
    "Experimental Setup": "The experimental evaluation covered two main aspects: surrogate learning performance and real-world hyperparameter optimization (HPO). For surrogate learning, two synthetic benchmark functions were used: the 2-fidelity Levy function and the 3-fidelity Branin function. Performance was measured using normalized root-mean-square-error (nRMSE) and mean-negative-log-likelihood (MNLL). For real-world applications, BMBO-DARN was tested on HPO for four popular machine learning models: Convolutional Neural Networks (CNN) for image classification (CIFAR-10), Online Latent Dirichlet Allocation (LDA) for text mining (20NewsGroups), XGBoost for diabetes diagnosis, and Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINN) for solving Burger's equation. Each application involved 2 or 3 distinct fidelities (e.g., number of training epochs, number of weak learners) with specified cost ratios. Initial training data typically consisted of 10 random hyperparameter settings per fidelity. BMBO-DARN's performance was compared against state-of-the-art multi-fidelity BO methods (MF-GP-UCB, MF-MES, SHTL, DNN-MFBO, MF-MES-Batch), single-fidelity BO methods (SF-Batch, SMAC3), and other HPO techniques (Hyperband, BOHB). All batch querying methods utilized a batch size of 5. The implementations were built using PyTorch and Hamiltorch for HMC sampling, with specific NN architectures and HMC parameters detailed.",
    "Limitations": "Existing multi-fidelity Bayesian optimization methods often suffer from oversimplifying or ignoring the complex, strong correlations between different fidelities, which can lead to inefficient surrogate learning and increased computational costs. The standard one-by-one querying strategy is unable to leverage parallel computing resources and risks selecting highly correlated or redundant queries. While the proposed batch acquisition function helps, its direct computation is challenging due to the mutual information calculation, and its optimization over a mixed space of continuous inputs and discrete fidelities naturally leads to a computationally expensive combinatorial search problem. Although the paper introduces an alternating optimization to mitigate this, it highlights an inherent complexity. Experimental observations also indicated that some GP-based baselines tended to get stuck in suboptimal hyperparameters, potentially due to less effective multi-fidelity information integration. The chosen posterior inference method, HMC, while accurate, can be computationally intensive.",
    "Future Research Directions": "The paper does not explicitly dedicate a section to future research. However, it implicitly suggests directions such as exploring other approximate inference approaches for Bayesian neural networks (e.g., variational inference, stochastic gradient HMC) to potentially improve scalability beyond Hamiltonian Monte Carlo. Further work could involve applying the proposed BMBO-DARN framework to a broader range of expensive black-box optimization problems beyond hyperparameter tuning. Additionally, enhancing the efficiency and robustness of the batch acquisition function's optimization, especially for scenarios with larger batch sizes or more complex fidelity structures, could be a valuable extension.",
    "Experiment Code": null,
    "Experiment Result": null

    "Title": "Supervising the Multi-Fidelity Race of Hyperparameter Configurations",
    "Main Contributions": "The paper introduces DyHPO, a novel Bayesian Optimization (BO) method for multi-fidelity hyperparameter optimization (HPO) that addresses sub-optimal budget allocation and poor rank correlation across different budgets. Its main contributions include a deep kernel for Gaussian Processes that embeds learning curve dynamics, a multi-budget acquisition function (EIMF), and a dynamic budget allocation strategy. DyHPO demonstrates statistically significant empirical gains over state-of-the-art HPO methods across 50 diverse datasets (Tabular, Image, NLP) and architectures (MLP, CNN/NAS, RNN), converging faster and more efficiently by identifying and promoting promising candidates.",
    "Methodology": "DyHPO employs a Gaussian Process (GP) surrogate model with a novel deep kernel. This deep kernel, `K(θ, w)`, takes as input the hyperparameter configuration `xi`, the budget `j`, and the past learning curve `Yi,j-1`. A neural network, `φ`, acts as a feature extractor, processing concatenated `xi` and normalized `j` through linear layers, and the learning curve `Yi,j-1` via a one-dimensional convolution followed by global max pooling. The outputs are then fed to a squared exponential (RBF) kernel `k`. Model parameters `θ` and `w` are optimized by maximizing marginal likelihood using Adam with a learning rate of 0.1. The acquisition function is a Multi-Fidelity Expected Improvement (EIMF), which extends the standard Expected Improvement by incorporating a budget-aware incumbent `ymax_j`. The algorithm dynamically selects configurations to train for one additional budget step based on `EIMF` and updates the surrogate model with observed validation scores.",
    "Experimental Setup": "Experiments were conducted on an Amazon EC2 M5 Instance (m5.xlarge), reporting mean regret and average rank over ten repetitions. Three benchmarks were used: LCBench (35 tabular datasets, 2,000 neural networks, 50 epochs, 7 numerical hyperparameters), TaskSet (12 NLP tasks, Adam8p search space, 8 continuous hyperparameters, scores every 200 iterations), and NAS-Bench-201 (CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, ImageNet, 15,625 architectures, 200 epochs, 6 categorical hyperparameters). Baselines included Random Search, HyperBand, BOHB, DEHB, ASHA, MF-DNN, and Dragonfly (BOCA). Preprocessing involved log-transforms for specific hyperparameters and MinMaxScaler for continuous ones. An ablation study compared DyHPO with and without the learning curve input to the kernel on NAS-Bench-201 datasets.",
    "Limitations": "The method lacks evaluation on very large deep learning models like Transformer-based architectures due to a scarcity of suitable tabular benchmarks. The pause and resume training procedure is only applicable to parametric models, meaning training for other models would need to be restarted. For small datasets with fast training times, the computational overhead of model-based techniques like DyHPO makes simpler approaches, such as random search, more practical and appealing. The authors also warn against running the method for extended periods for only marginal performance gains, unless mission-critical, due to energy consumption.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research should focus on developing and utilizing tabular benchmarks for hyperparameter optimization of very large deep learning models, such as Transformers. Investigating methods to adapt the pause/resume training procedure for non-parametric models or exploring alternative strategies when restarts are unavoidable would also be beneficial. Further work could also involve reducing the overhead of model-based HPO techniques for small, fast-training datasets or defining clear criteria for when complex solutions like DyHPO are justified. The community is also encouraged to create sparse benchmarks with surrogates to save energy, as opposed to dense tabular ones.",

self.model.parameters(), 'lr': self.configuration['learning_rate']},\n            {'params': self.feature_extractor.parameters(), 'lr': self.configuration['learning_rate']}],\n        )\n        X_train = data['X_train']\n        train_budgets = data['train_budgets']\n        train_curves = data['train_curves']\n        y_train = data['y_train']\n        initial_state = self.get_state()\n        training_errored = False\n        if self.restart:\n            self.restart_optimization()\n            nr_epochs = self.nr_epochs\n            if self.initial_nr_points <= self.iterations:\n                self.restart = False\n        else:\n            nr_epochs = self.refine_epochs\n        mse = 0.0\n        for epoch_nr in range(0, nr_epochs):\n            nr_examples_batch = X_train.size(dim=0)\n            if nr_examples_batch == 1:\n                continue\n            self.optimizer.zero_grad()\n            projected_x = self.feature_extractor(X_train, train_budgets, train_curves)\n            self.model.set_train_data(projected_x, y_train, strict=False)\n            output = self.model(projected_x)\n            try:\n                loss = -self.mll(output, self.model.train_targets)\n                loss_value = loss.detach().to('cpu').item()\n                mse = gpytorch.metrics.mean_squared_error(output, self.model.train_targets)\n                self.logger.debug(\n                    f'Epoch {epoch_nr} - MSE {mse:.5f}, '\n                    f'Loss: {loss_value:.3f}, '\n                    f'lengthscale: {self.model.covar_module.base_kernel.lengthscale.item():.3f}, '\n                    f'noise: {self.model.likelihood.noise.item():.3f}, '\n                )\n                loss.backward()\n                self.optimizer.step()\n            except Exception as training_error:\n                self.logger.error(f'The following error happened while training: {training_error}')\n                self.restart = True\n                training_errored = True\n                break\n        if training_errored:\n            self.save_checkpoint(initial_state)\n            self.load_checkpoint()\n    def predict_pipeline(\n        self,\n        train_data: Dict[str, torch.Tensor],\n        test_data: Dict[str, torch.Tensor],\n    ) -> Tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:\n        self.model.eval()\n        self.feature_extractor.eval()\n        self.likelihood.eval()\n        with torch.no_grad():\n            projected_train_x = self.feature_extractor(\n                train_data['X_train'],\n                train_data['train_budgets'],\n                train_data['train_curves'],\n            )\n            self.model.set_train_data(inputs=projected_train_x, targets=train_data['y_train'], strict=False)\n            projected_test_x = self.feature_extractor(\n                test_data['X_test'],\n                test_data['test_budgets'],\n                test_data['test_curves'],\n            )\n            preds = self.likelihood(self.model(projected_test_x))\n        means = preds.mean.detach().to('cpu').numpy().reshape(-1, )\n        stds = preds.stddev.detach().to('cpu').numpy().reshape(-1, )\n        return means, stds\n    def restart_optimization():\n        self.feature_extractor = FeatureExtractor(self.configuration).to(self.dev)\n        self.model, self.likelihood, self.mll = \n            self.get_model_likelihood_mll(\n                self.configuration[f'layer{self.feature_extractor.nr_layers}_units'],\n            )\n        self.optimizer = torch.optim.Adam([\n            {'params': self.model.parameters(), 'lr': self.configuration['learning_rate']},\n            {'params': self.feature_extractor.parameters(), 'lr': self.configuration['learning_rate']}],\n        )\nclass DyHPOAlgorithm:\n    def __init__(        self,        hp_candidates: np.ndarray,        log_indicator: List,        seed: int = 11,        max_benchmark_epochs: int = 52,        fantasize_step: int = 1,        minimization: bool = True,        total_budget: int = 500,        device: str = None,        dataset_name: str = 'unknown',        output_path: str = '.',        surrogate_config: dict = None,        verbose: bool = True,    ):        torch.backends.cudnn.deterministic = True        torch.backends.cudnn.benchmark = False        torch.manual_seed(seed)        np.random.seed(seed)        if device is None:            self.dev = torch.device(                'cuda') if torch.cuda.is_available() else torch.device('cpu')        else:            self.dev = torch.device(device)\n        self.hp_candidates = hp_candidates\n        self.log_indicator = log_indicator\n        self.scaler = MinMaxScaler()\n        self.hp_candidates = self.preprocess_hp_candidates()\n        self.minimization = minimization\n        self.seed = seed\n        if verbose:\n            logging_level = logging.DEBUG\n        else:\n            logging_level = logging.INFO\n        self.logger = logging.getLogger()\n        logging.basicConfig(\n            format='%(levelname)s:%(asctime)s:%(message)s',\n            filename=f'dyhpo_surrogate_{dataset_name}_{seed}.log',\n            level=logging_level,\n        )\n        self.examples = dict()\n        self.performances = dict()\n        torch.manual_seed(seed)\n        np.random.seed(seed)\n        self.max_benchmark_epochs = max_benchmark_epochs\n        self.total_budget = total_budget\n        self.fantasize_step = fantasize_step\n        self.nr_features = self.hp_candidates.shape[1]\n        initial_configurations_nr = 1\n        conf_individual_budget = 1\n        self.init_conf_indices = np.random.choice(self.hp_candidates.shape[0], initial_configurations_nr, replace=False)\n        self.init_budgets = [conf_individual_budget] * initial_configurations_nr\n        self.fraction_random_configs = 0.1\n        self.model = None\n        self.initial_random_index = 0\n        if surrogate_config is None:\n            self.surrogate_config = {\n                'nr_layers': 2,\n                'nr_initial_features': self.nr_features,\n                'layer1_units': 64,\n                'layer2_units': 128,\n                'cnn_nr_channels': 4,\n                'cnn_kernel_size': 3,\n                'batch_size': 64,\n                'nr_epochs': 1000,\n                'nr_patience_epochs': 10,\n                'learning_rate': 0.001,\n            }\n        else:\n            self.surrogate_config = surrogate_config\n        self.best_value_observed = np.NINF\n        self.diverged_configs = set()\n        self.info_dict = dict()\n        self.suggest_time_duration = 0\n        self.budget_spent = 0\n        self.output_path = output_path\n        self.dataset_name = dataset_name\n        self.no_improvement_threshold = int(self.max_benchmark_epochs + 0.2 * self.max_benchmark_epochs)\n        self.no_improvement_patience = 0\n    def _train_surrogate(self):\n        data = self._prepare_dataset_and_budgets()\n        self.logger.info(f'Started training the model')\n        self.model.train_pipeline(\n            data,\n            load_checkpoint=False,\n        )\n    def _predict(self) -> Tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray, List, List]:\n        configurations, hp_indices, budgets, learning_curves = self.generate_candidate_configurations()\n        budgets = np.array(budgets, dtype=np.single)\n        non_scaled_budgets = copy.deepcopy(budgets)\n        budgets = budgets / self.max_benchmark_epochs\n        configurations = np.array(configurations, dtype=np.single)\n        configurations = torch.tensor(configurations)\n        configurations = configurations.to(device=self.dev)\n        budgets = torch.tensor(budgets)\n        budgets = budgets.to(device=self.dev)\n        learning_curves = self.patch_curves_to_same_length(learning_curves)\n        learning_curves = np.array(learning_curves, dtype=np.single)\n        learning_curves = torch.tensor(learning_curves)\n        learning_curves = learning_curves.to(device=self.dev)\n        train_data = self._prepare_dataset_and_budgets()\n        test_data = {\n            'X_test': configurations,\n            'test_budgets': budgets,\n            'test_curves': learning_curves,\n        }\n        mean_predictions, std_predictions = self.model.predict_pipeline(train_data, test_data)\n        return mean_predictions, std_predictions, hp_indices, non_scaled_budgets\n    def suggest(self) -> Tuple[int, int]:\n        suggest_time_start = time.time()\n        if self.initial_random_index < len(self.init_conf_indices):\n            self.logger.info(\n                'Not enough configurations to build a model. '\n                'Returning randomly sampled configuration'\n            )\n            random_indice = self.init_conf_indices[self.initial_random_index]\n            budget = self.init_budgets[self.initial_random_index]\n            self.initial_random_index += 1\n            return random_indice, budget\n        else:\n            mean_predictions, std_predictions, hp_indices, non_scaled_budgets = self._predict()\n            best_prediction_index = self.find_suggested_config(\n                mean_predictions,\n                std_predictions,\n                non_scaled_budgets,\n            )\n            best_config_index = hp_indices[best_prediction_index]\n            if best_config_index in self.examples:\n                evaluated_budgets = self.examples[best_config_index]\n                max_budget = max(evaluated_budgets)\n                budget = max_budget + self.fantasize_step\n                if budget > self.max_benchmark_epochs:\n                    budget = self.max_benchmark_epochs\n            else:\n                budget = self.fantasize_step\n        suggest_time_end = time.time()\n        self.suggest_time_duration = suggest_time_end - suggest_time_start\n        self.budget_spent += self.fantasize_step\n        if self.budget_spent > self.total_budget:\n            exit(0)\n        return best_config_index, budget\n    def observe(\n        self,\n        hp_index: int,\n        b: int,\n        learning_curve: np.ndarray,\n        alg_time: Optional[float] = None,\n    ):\n        score = learning_curve[-1]\n        if np.isnan(learning_curve).any():\n            self.update_info_dict(hp_index, b, np.nan, 0)\n            self.diverged_configs.add(hp_index)\n            return\n        observe_time_start = time.time()\n        self.examples[hp_index] = np.arange(1, b + 1).tolist()\n        self.performances[hp_index] = learning_curve\n        if self.best_value_observed < score:\n            self.best_value_observed = score\n            self.no_improvement_patience = 0\n        else:\n            self.no_improvement_patience += 1\n        observe_time_end = time.time()\n        train_time_duration = 0\n        if self.initial_random_index >= len(self.init_conf_indices):\n            train_time_start = time.time()\n            if self.model is None:\n                self.model = DyHPO(\n                    self.surrogate_config,\n                    self.dev,\n                    self.dataset_name,\n                    self.output_path,\n                    self.seed,\n                )\n            if self.no_improvement_patience == self.no_improvement_threshold:\n                self.model.restart = True\n            self._train_surrogate()\n            train_time_end = time.time()\n            train_time_duration = train_time_end - train_time_start\n        observe_time_duration = observe_time_end - observe_time_start\n        total_duration = observe_time_duration + self.suggest_time_duration + train_time_duration\n        if alg_time is not None:\n            total_duration = total_duration + alg_time\n        self.update_info_dict(hp_index, b, score, total_duration)\n    def history_configurations(\n        self,\n    ) -> Tuple[List, List, List, List]:\n        train_examples = []\n        train_labels = []\n        train_budgets = []\n        train_curves = []\n        for hp_index in self.examples:\n            budgets = self.examples[hp_index]\n            performances = self.performances[hp_index]\n            example = self.hp_candidates[hp_index]\n            for budget, performance in zip(budgets, performances):\n                train_examples.append(example)\n                train_budgets.append(budget)\n                train_labels.append(performance)\n                train_curve = performances[:budget - 1] if budget > 1 else [0.0]\n                difference_curve_length = self.surrogate_config['cnn_kernel_size'] - len(train_curve)\n                if difference_curve_length > 0:\n                    train_curve.extend([0.0] * difference_curve_length)\n                train_curves.append(train_curve)\n        return train_examples, train_labels, train_budgets, train_curves\n    def acq(\n        self,\n        best_value: float,\n        mean: float,\n        std: float,\n        explore_factor: Optional[float] = 0.25,\n        acq_fc: str = 'ei',\n    ) -> float:\n        if acq_fc == 'ei':\n            if std == 0:\n                return 0\n            z = (mean - best_value) / std\n            acq_value = (mean - best_value) * norm.cdf(z) + std * norm.pdf(z)\n        elif acq_fc == 'ucb':\n            acq_value = mean + explore_factor * std\n        elif acq_fc == 'thompson':\n            acq_value = np.random.normal(mean, std)\n        elif acq_fc == 'exploit':\n            acq_value = mean\n        else:\n            raise NotImplementedError(\n                f'Acquisition function {acq_fc} has not been'\n                f'implemented',\n            )\n        return acq_value\n    def find_suggested_config(\n        self,\n        mean_predictions: np.ndarray,\n        mean_stds: np.ndarray,\n        budgets: List,\n    ) -> int:\n        highest_acq_value = np.NINF\n        best_index = -1\n        index = 0\n        for mean_value, std in zip(mean_predictions, mean_stds):\n            budget = int(budgets[index])\n            best_value = self.calculate_fidelity_ymax(budget)\n            acq_value = self.acq(best_value, mean_value, std, acq_fc='ei')\n            if acq_value > highest_acq_value:\n                highest_acq_value = acq_value\n                best_index = index\n            index += 1\n        return best_index\n    def calculate_fidelity_ymax(self, fidelity: int):\n        exact_fidelity_config_values = []\n        lower_fidelity_config_values = []\n        for example_index in self.examples.keys():\n            try:\n                performance = self.performances[example_index][fidelity - 1]\n                exact_fidelity_config_values.append(performance)\n            except IndexError:\n                learning_curve = self.performances[example_index]\n                lower_fidelity_config_values.append(max(learning_curve))\n        if len(exact_fidelity_config_values) > 0:\n            best_value = max(exact_fidelity_config_values)\n        else:\n            best_value = max(lower_fidelity_config_values)\n        return best_value\n    def preprocess_hp_candidates(self) -> List:\n        log_hp_candidates = []\n        for hp_candidate in self.hp_candidates:\n            new_hp_candidate = []\n            for index, hp_value in enumerate(hp_candidate):\n                new_hp_candidate.append(math.log(hp_value) if self.log_indicator[index] else hp_value)\n            log_hp_candidates.append(new_hp_candidate)\n        log_hp_candidates = np.array(log_hp_candidates)\n        log_hp_candidates = self.scaler.fit_transform(log_hp_candidates)\n        return log_hp_candidates\n    @staticmethod\n    def patch_curves_to_same_length(curves):\n        max_curve_length = 0\n        for curve in curves:\n            if len(curve) > max_curve_length:\n                max_curve_length = len(curve)\n        for curve in curves:\n            difference = max_curve_length - len(curve)\n            if difference > 0:\n                curve.extend([0.0] * difference)\n        return curves",
    "Experiment Result": "### Surrogate Model Configuration:\n- **Neural Network Architecture (Feature Extractor)**:\n  - `nr_layers`: 2\n  - `nr_initial_features`: Determined by the number of hyperparameters\n  - `layer1_units`: 64\n  - `layer2_units`: 128\n  - `cnn_nr_channels`: 4\n  - `cnn_kernel_size`: 3\n- **Training Parameters for Surrogate Model**:\n  - `batch_size`: 64\n  - `nr_epochs`: 1000\n  - `nr_patience_epochs`: 10 (early stopping)\n  - `learning_rate`: 0.001 (for Adam optimizer)\n  - `refine_epochs`: 50 (epochs for refining the model after initial full training/restart)\n- **Optimizer**: Adam is used for optimizing both the GP and Feature Extractor parameters.\n\n### HPO Algorithm Parameters:\n- **Seed**: An integer chosen from a range of 0-9 (controlled by `args.index` in `main_experiment.py`).\n- **`max_benchmark_epochs`**: 51 (for both LCBench and TaskSet benchmarks).\n- **`fantasize_step`**: 1 (the number of budget steps to look ahead).\n- **`total_budget`**: 1000 (total budget given for hyperparameter optimization).\n- **`minimization`**: `True` for `TaskSet` (loss minimization), `False` for `LCBench` (accuracy maximization).\n- **Initial Configurations**: 1 configuration chosen randomly, evaluated for 1 budget step (`initial_configurations_nr = 1`, `conf_individual_budget = 1`).\n- **Random Configuration Fraction**: 0.1 (fraction of configurations to be taken randomly, though initial points are explicitly handled).\n- **Acquisition Function**: Multi-Fidelity Expected Improvement (EIMF), implemented as `'ei'` in `acq` function, incorporating `calculate_fidelity_ymax` for budget-aware incumbent.\n- **Model Restart/Refinement**: The model restarts its optimization (reinitializes FeatureExtractor and GP) if `no_improvement_patience` reaches `no_improvement_threshold`, where `no_improvement_threshold = int(max_benchmark_epochs + 0.2 * max_benchmark_epochs)`.\n\n### Data Preprocessing:\n- **Hyperparameters**: Scaled using `MinMaxScaler`. Log-transformed for hyperparameters indicated as log-sampled.\n- **Budgets**: Normalized by `max_benchmark_epochs` to a [0, 1] range for input to the FeatureExtractor.\n- **Learning Curves**: Padded with zeros to a consistent length equal to `cnn_kernel_size` if shorter than kernel size.\n\n### Benchmarks:\n- **LCBench**: Uses `Train/val_balanced_accuracy` (maximization objective).\n- **TaskSet**: Uses `valid/loss` (minimization objective)."
}{
    "Title": "Supervising the Multi-Fidelity Race of Hyperparameter Configurations",
    "Main Contributions": "DyHPO addresses the sub-optimal budget allocation and poor rank correlation issues in multi-fidelity hyperparameter optimization (HPO). It introduces a novel Bayesian Optimization (BO) method based on Gaussian Processes (GP) with a deep kernel that explicitly embeds learning curve dynamics and a new multi-fidelity acquisition function. This dynamic approach significantly outperforms state-of-the-art HPO methods across diverse deep learning architectures and 50 datasets, enabling more efficient and robust HPO by dynamically deciding which configuration to train further in a race among all feasible configurations.",
    "Methodology": "DyHPO is a Bayesian Optimization algorithm that employs a Gaussian Process surrogate model with several key components: 1. A **Deep Multi-Fidelity Surrogate** utilizes a deep kernel, where a neural network (feature extractor φ) processes hyperparameter configurations, normalized budget information, and the historical learning curve (via 1D convolution and max-pooling). This allows the GP to capture learning dynamics across different fidelities. 2. A **Multi-Fidelity Expected Improvement (EIMF)** acquisition function extends the standard Expected Improvement by considering the best observed performance for a specific budget or globally if no budget-specific data exists. 3. The **DyHPO Algorithm** dynamically selects the most promising candidate configuration to train for one additional budget step using EIMF and updates the surrogate model with the new observations. This ensures configurations are explored by slowly increasing their budget.",
    "Experimental Setup": "DyHPO was evaluated on hyperparameter optimization (HPO) tasks for tabular, text, and image classification, running experiments on an Amazon EC2 M5 Instance (m5.xlarge). Performance was measured by mean regret and average rank over ten repetitions, with statistical significance assessed using the Friedman test and Wilcoxon signed-rank test (α = 0.05). Three benchmarks were utilized: LCBench (35 tabular datasets, 50 epochs, 7 hyperparameters for MLPs), TaskSet (subset of 12 NLP tasks, Adam8p search space for RNNs), and NAS-Bench-201 (3 image datasets, 15,625 architectures, 6 categorical hyperparameters for CNN/NAS, 200 epochs). Baselines included Random Search, HyperBand, BOHB, DEHB, ASHA, MF-DNN, and Dragonfly (BOCA). Preprocessing involved log-transforms and Min-Max scaling for numerical hyperparameters, and one-hot encoding or baseline-specific methods for categorical ones.",
    "Limitations": "The identified limitations include: 1. A lack of tabular benchmarks for Hyperparameter Optimization (HPO) on very large deep learning models like Transformers, making it difficult to fully evaluate DyHPO in this domain. 2. The pause-and-resume training procedure is only applicable to parametric models; non-parametric models would require restarting training from scratch. 3. For small, fast-training datasets, the computational overhead of model-based techniques like DyHPO might render simpler approaches, such as random search, more efficient and practically appealing.",
    "Future Research Directions": "Future research directions include extending and evaluating DyHPO on very large deep learning models, specifically Transformers-based architectures, to test its scalability and performance in these computationally intensive settings. The paper also encourages the community to develop sparse benchmarks with surrogates, rather than dense tabular ones, to promote energy efficiency in future HPO research.",
    "Experiment Code": "class DyHPOAlgorithm:\n\n    def __init__(\n        self,\n        hp_candidates: np.ndarray,\n        log_indicator: List,\n        seed: int = 11,\n  The name of the dataset that the experiment will be run on.\n            output_path: str\n                The path where all the output will be stored.\n            surrogate_config: dict\n                The model configurations for the surrogate.\n            verbose: boolean\n                If detailed information is preferred in the log file.\n        \"\"\"\n        torch.backends.cudnn.deterministic = True\n        torch.backends.cudnn.benchmark = False\n        torch.manual_seed(seed)\n        np.random.seed(seed)\n\n        if device is None:\n level=logging_level,\n        )\n\n        # the keys will be hyperparameter indices while the value\n        # will be a list with all the budgets evaluated for examples\n        # and with all of the ensemble\n        torch.manual_seed(seed)\n        np.random.seed(seed)\n\n        self.max_benchmark_epochs = max_benchmark_epochs\n        self.total_budget = total_budget\n        self.fantasize_step = fantasize_step\n        self.nr_features = self.hp_candidates.shape[1]\n\n        initial_configurations_nr = 1\n        conf_individual_budget = 1\n    # with what percentage configurations will be taken randomly instead of being sampled from the model\n        self.fraction_random_configs = 0.1\n\n        self.model = None\n        # An index
